Pistons, flows, explosions and a wither can't move or break one: they join our blocks in `CustomBlockListener`'s guards, and the old handlers that popped the prize onto the ground are gone. A head placed before 0.37.3 has no placer and breaks for anyone.

**Hub only.** The Arcade Machine is the one placeable Arcade block (`/hcm give arcade`, `recipes.arcade`, `skins.arcade`). The old Crate Machine / Scratch-Ticket Booth / Pity Exchange / Token Counter blocks are no longer given; any already placed open the matching hub screen (a Crate Machine opens its crate, or the list of every crate when there are several). A tap on any of these that arrives already cancelled (vanilla spawn protection, a protection plugin's "use" rule; both spare ops) still opens (`CustomBlockListener.opensWhenCancelled`, the join signs' policy: the handler listens without `ignoreCancelled` and reads `useInteractedBlock() == DENY` first), behind the economy sandbox and the build-rights check as before, with one console line a player a day, written only once one of our screens really opened and naming no cause ("by the server or another plugin": a spectator or an item cooling down arrives cancelled too); a cancelled tap on any other block of ours, or by a player in a world session (the session guard denies every block and every screen but the game's), is left alone. A Bedrock tap on a block of ours never puts a hat on (`PrizeItemListener.wearsOnBlockTap`'s `ours`: a placed HomeCraft block, a join sign, or a skull carrying a Mini, placed or wild, which `MiniHeadListener` catches or shows): the Arcade Machine and a Mini are player heads, not interactable, so a tap used to open the screen or catch the Mini and wear the hat in hand too.

**Physical home — the Arcade at the Mall:** an anchor tenant alongside the Museum & Auction House, where a big pull is an *event*. *(Framed as an Arcade, not a casino: earned tokens, not real money.)*

### 3.10 The Courier — Deliveries (NEW)

A **Site on the PC** (§2.2) that pays players to move things around the map. It exists because §3.1's
market is a *finite, conserved* exchange: selling into it moves stock and moves the price, so it cannot
be the only way a kid earns money without either the price collapsing or the catalog growing into a
faucet — which §3.1 explicitly refuses. The Courier is the deliberate alternative: **effort in the
world, paid in money, with nothing produced and nothing sold.**

**The loop.** Open the board, pick a distance band, take a run. A **waypoint** is rolled then and there
— a random bearing and distance, resolved to the highest solid block, rejected and re-rolled if it is
liquid, an ocean biome, or land the player has no build rights to (up to `waypoint.max_rerolls`; after
that the run is simply not offered, because a drop-off in the middle of an ocean is worse than a short
board). Walk there, click **Hand it over** within `turn_in_radius` blocks, get paid. One run at a time,
per player.

**What it pays.** `fee = (base + per_block × distance) × travel_multiplier`. Distance is the
**horizontal straight line** from where the job was accepted to the waypoint, **clamped to
`[min_distance, max_distance]`** and **locked at acceptance** — so going the long way round, or picking
a fight on the way, earns nothing extra and the fee cannot be re-priced by anything that happens en
route. At the shipped `base: 4.0` / `per_block: 0.012`, the five daily runs at their band midpoints come
to **about $93 on foot** — a day's honest income that sits *below* what the same time spent mining and
selling returns, which is the point: the Courier is a floor under a slow day, not a better grind.

**The travel multiplier is the mechanism.** Every movement statistic is snapshotted when the job is
accepted and diffed at turn-in, then **blended by the fraction of centimetres moved in each group** —
never by a total, and never by whichever vehicle was touched last. That distinction is what makes it
un-gameable in both directions: a player who walks the route once and one who walks it four times both
score **1.00**, so padding the trip is worthless, and someone who rides half and walks half lands
**between** the two multipliers rather than at the better one. Vanilla's accounting is mutually
exclusive — exactly one counter increments per tick — so the fractions are a real partition and cannot
sum to more than the distance covered. `FALL_ONE_CM` is deliberately in **no** group (falling is not
travelling) and the denominator is the grouped statistics only, so an ungrouped counter can never
dilute a multiplier.

| Group | Statistics | Shipped |
|---|---|---|
| `foot` | WALK / SPRINT / CROUCH / SWIM / WALK_ON_WATER / WALK_UNDER_WATER / CLIMB | **1.00** |
| `mount` | HORSE / STRIDER / PIG / NAUTILUS | 0.85 |
| `boat` | BOAT | 0.80 |
| `ghast` | HAPPY_GHAST | 0.70 |
| `rail` | MINECART | 0.65 |
| `elytra` | AVIATE | 0.45 |
| `creative` | FLY | **0.00** |

**A Nautilus is grouped with the mounts, not the boats.** It reads as water travel, but the API says
otherwise: `AbstractNautilus` is `Tameable, InventoryHolder, Vehicle` with an
`ArmoredSaddledMountInventory` — a saddled, armoured, tameable mount like a Strider, and priced like
one. It is one line of config if that call turns out to feel wrong in play. `HAPPY_GHAST_ONE_CM` and
`NAUTILUS_ONE_CM` are resolved **by name** rather than referenced directly, because the pinned API may
predate them: a server without one simply does not count it, and neither case needs a code change.

**Anti-teleport.** If total tracked movement comes to less than `anti_teleport.floor` (70%) of the
locked distance, the travel fee pays `anti_teleport.payout` (20%) instead. Cargo on a trade run still
pays in full, because that half is a real sale. **A zero blend is 0.00, not 1.00** — a player who
arrives having moved nothing is not handed the on-foot rate by default. It is logged at `FINE` and
never called out in chat: a nether-portal shortcut is not cheating, it is just not walking it, and the
smaller number is the whole message.

**Trade runs.** Right-click a band with market cargo in hand and the run carries it: the stack is
quoted at acceptance, and at the drop-off it is **sold through the market for real** — stock moves +N,
daily sell limits apply, commission applies, exactly as if it had been sold at home. It is paid at the
**live** rate, **not the locked quote**. The quote is recorded on the job row and shown on the board
for reference only. Paying the locked figure would mint the difference, and **only the travel fee is
meant to be new money**. A trade run refuses at turn-in if the cargo is no longer in the inventory.

**Caps and expiry.** Three bands — `local` 200–600 (2/day), `regional` 600–1800 (2/day), `long_haul`
1800–4000 (1/day) — keyed by **UTC epoch-day at read time**, the house pattern (§8: no scheduler).
Runs expire after `expire_minutes` (60); a sweep closes stale ones every minute so the band slot comes
back. **Abandoning does not spend the slot** — the day's count is ACTIVE + DELIVERED, so dropping a run
you can't finish costs nothing but the walk. Every accept and every turn-in goes through the economy
sandbox (§11 #1): there is no courier money in a creative world.

**The destination (Phase 2).** A delivery now arrives somewhere rather than at a coordinate. Once the
player is within `building.place_at_blocks` of the waypoint a **small house** goes up with a
**villager** outside it; right-click them to hand the crate over. When the run ends — delivered,
expired, abandoned, or the plugin shutting down — **the field is put back exactly as it was.**

*Houses are vanilla.* Mojang already maintains a correct-looking small village house per biome, so the
shipped table names those structure keys rather than shipping schematics: `village/<family>/houses/…`
across plains, desert, savanna, taiga and snowy, with everything unmapped (jungle, swamp, mangrove)
falling to plains. **Every key is resolved once at startup** and any that no longer exists is dropped
with a warning naming it, because Mojang renames these between versions and the alternative is a
delivery that fails after the player has walked eighteen hundred blocks. Rotation is random from the
four quarter-turns — free variety, no extra assets.

*Trees are cleared; hills are refused.* Ground level is found by looking **down** through leaves,
trunks and undergrowth to real terrain (`Ground`), not by reading a heightmap — **every heightmap
Minecraft keeps answers a different question**. `MOTION_BLOCKING`, which is what `getHighestBlockYAt`
returns by default, is "the highest block that blocks motion or holds a fluid", so in a wood it
reports the canopy and beside a lake the water surface. `OCEAN_FLOOR` is no better: its predicate is
`blocksMotion()`, which leaves satisfy. `MOTION_BLOCKING_NO_LEAVES` drops the leaves and still stops
on a trunk. There is no heightmap that means "the ground", so the walk down is the only honest answer
— and it is also the only thing that can tell "a tree is in the way" (clear it) from "this is a lake"
(deliver elsewhere), which look identical from above and need opposite answers.

Whole trees, not slices. The clearing is a **flood fill** through connected logs and leaves from
everything rooted in the building's footprint, and **the captured region grows to bound it**
(`DeliverySite.unionAxis`). Cutting at a fixed height left canopy hanging in the sky, and that is
worse than it looks: orphaned leaves **decay**, decay happens whether or not the block was inside
the region, and a restore cannot put back what Minecraft deleted on its own. Caps (`max_clear_blocks`,
`max_region_side`) stop one delivery in a dark forest asking to snapshot half a chunk; when a cap
bites, whatever falls outside the region is **left standing rather than cleared**, because a few
leaves hanging for an hour is cosmetic and a block changed outside the snapshot is permanent.

Real relief still refuses: ground varying by more than `max_slope` across the footprint, or a
footprint more than `max_liquid_percent` water, is rerolled. Terraforming somebody's hillside cannot
be undone by putting blocks back; picking a different field can. Vanilla puts villages in forests
constantly, so a wood is a buildable field — the growth inside the region is cleared before placement
and restored with everything else afterwards. A shallow gap under the house is closed by a foundation
layer of the local surface material, filled only downward under blocks the structure placed and only
inside the snapshotted region.

*The terrain test runs while the waypoint is chosen*, not only at placement. Terrain does not change
during a delivery — a canopy will not grow in an hour — so a field that cannot be built on costs a
reroll rather than a walk. Only the container scan stays at placement, because somebody really can
put a chest down in the meantime. When placement is refused anyway, it is refused **once**, logged
with the reason (`building.debug`), and the player is told at the waypoint rather than left to find
an empty field.

*The undo record is the whole design.* Before the first block changes, the region is captured as a
**palette-plus-indices blob, GZIPped, into SQLite** (`courier_sites`) — a 28×28×16 box of mostly air
compresses to almost nothing, which is why this needs **no WorldEdit dependency**: the undo record
lives in the same database, the same transaction and the same backup as the job it belongs to. The row
is deleted **only once the restore has actually run**. A restore that cannot run right now — the usual
case, because a player who gives up walks home and the chunk unloads behind them — is parked as
`RESTORE_PENDING` and picked up by the next `ChunkLoadEvent` for that region, or failing that by the
**sweep on the next plugin enable**, which is the first thing the module does. A house that outlives
the plugin that knows how to remove it is the one outcome this is built to make impossible.

*Two things the building is not.* It is not **salvage** — edits inside a standing site are refused, both
because mining it would hand out free blocks the restore then deletes and because every changed block
is one the snapshot no longer describes. And it is not **loot**: village templates ship chests carrying
loot tables, and a building that reappears at the end of every run would turn that into a per-delivery
**item faucet** in an economy whose whole premise is that material enters only when somebody mines it —
so the furniture stays and the contents do not.

*What a site refuses to be built over.* One rule, four cases: **a snapshot restores block data and
nothing else**, so anything whose value lives elsewhere has to be refused rather than built over,
because the restore that makes the rest of this safe does not reach it.
- **Blocks with contents.** A chest is not recoverable from its block data. Somebody's unclaimed
  storage is still somebody's.
- **Entities that were placed.** Item frames, armour stands, chest minecarts, boats, displays. These are
  not in the snapshot *at all*, so anything that destroys one during the job destroys it for good.
  Wandering mobs are ignored — refusing a field because a cow walked through it would refuse most
  fields — which is why the test names armour stands before it exempts living entities, and why a horse
  (a `Vehicle` *and* an `InventoryHolder`) is deliberately not caught.
- **Anything HomeCraft already tracks there.** One query across the six tables that key on
  `(world, x, y, z)`. The case that matters is a **placed Mini**: a numbered, capped, uniquely-minted
  collectible. The snapshot would dutifully restore the head; it would not restore the owner's access
  during the job, and a failure in that window loses a copy that cannot be re-minted.
- **Block types only the world knows about** (`building.avoid_blocks`, player heads by default) — a head
  in a field is either a decoration or a death-storage grave, and neither should spend an hour behind a
  wall. Chest- and armour-stand-based graves are already caught by the two cases above.

The cheap half of that — the tracked-placement query and the entity sweep — also runs **while the
waypoint is being rolled**, so a bad spot is rerolled onto a different field rather than becoming a job
the player walks to and finds empty. The full block scan is thousands of reads, so it runs once, at
placement, where it is also the last word: an hour is long enough for somebody to put a chest down.

*The capture box is centred on where the building is put*, not on the waypoint — the two differ by
half the structure. Since Bukkit does not say which corner a rotation pivots around, the structure can
land in any quadrant around its placement origin, so the box is sized `(span + padding) × 2 + 1` about
that origin, which provably contains every rotation. `CaptureBoxTest` pins it: a block outside the
snapshot is captured by nothing and restored by nothing, so it stays in the world for good.

*The villager is a fixture, not a mob.* No AI, invulnerable, silent, persistent, profession matched to
the biome family, PDC-tagged with the job id. Right-click opens the hand-over and **never a trade
window** — a courier villager with vanilla trades would be an emerald pipeline no part of this economy
accounts for. Anyone else who clicks them gets a line of flavour text.

They spawn **facing away from the building**, which is to say towards whoever walks up, and rotation
still works with the AI off — so they turn to watch a nearby player, make an occasional noise, and say
something on hand-over. That is the cheap half of being alive; without it they read as a prop.

*Worldgen scaffolding is stripped after placement.* Village pieces are assembled by the jigsaw
generator, which consumes each `JIGSAW` block and replaces it with a recorded final state.
`Structure.place` runs none of that, so the markers survive as real, visible, op-interactable blocks —
one stood beside a front door on the first live delivery. They become air: **Bukkit exposes no way to
read the recorded final state** (`org.bukkit.block.Jigsaw` is an empty marker interface), and for the
village connectors these templates carry, air is what the generator would have left anyway.

*The building comes down when the player leaves, not on a clock.* Three rules, in order of how much
they matter: **never while somebody is inside the footprint** — restoring logs into the space a player
occupies suffocates them, and that defers even past the backstop; not before `linger_seconds`, so it
does not vanish in the same breath as the payout; then once nobody is within `restore_distance` (it is
out of sight, so it simply is not there next time they look) or `max_linger_seconds` has elapsed, which
covers somebody logging off on the doorstep. A timer alone was tried and is wrong by construction: it
can always run out while the player is standing in the doorway, which is what it did.

*The PC hand-in never goes away.* If placement fails for any reason — no template resolved, no flat
ground, a claim appeared since the waypoint was chosen — the run is still completable from the job
board at the drop-off. Nobody gets stranded four thousand blocks from home because a structure did not
paste.

**The package.** A courier run hands you a **crate** to carry — a player head, textured per band so
it reads as the size of the trip, and never an untextured one: a blank skin is a default Steve head,
so a band with no texture of its own borrows another band's — and the recipient wants it **in your hand**, not merely owned.
Trade runs get none: their cargo is the player's own goods, which is the point of that kind of run.

*It is worth nothing and must stay worth nothing.* Anything that survives its job is an item you can
mint on demand by taking Courier work, in an economy whose premise is that material enters only when
somebody mines it. So it is **unplaceable, unwearable, unstackable, unlistable**, cannot become cargo
for a second job, and is **destroyed the moment the delivery ends by any route** — handed over,
abandoned, expired, or the job gone entirely. A crate whose job is over is removed on sight, which is
also the backstop for any path that forgets to tidy up.

*Kept strictly apart from Minis.* A package is a player head; so is a Mini. They are told apart by
their PDC keys and never by looking like a head — a Mini by the `MINI_*` family, a package by
`hcm:courier_package` and nothing else. So a package is never minted, never given a serial, never in
the Museum, never counted in circulation, appraised or auctionable: every one of those paths asks for
a Mini reference, and a package has none.

*Unwearable is done in the item, not in events.* A player head is a helmet, and there are a great many
ways to put a hat on — the armour slot, shift-click, the hotbar swap key, right-clicking in the air, a
dispenser, an armour stand. The crate's own `equippable` component moves its slot off the head, which
disables all of them at once, wherever the item ends up. The armour-slot click is guarded as a second
line, because that component is a newer API than the plugin's floor and `PlayerArmorChangeEvent`
cannot be cancelled.

*Where it had to be refused, and where it did not.* The audit is smaller than it looks: the market
catalog does not buy player heads, the Auction and Vending House both demand a Mini reference, and the
Printer consumes a Card — so those reject a crate structurally, without a line of code. The genuinely
open surface is the **Marketplace Pallet**, which accepts an arbitrary held stack, and that is where
the explicit refusal lives, alongside the one for trade-run cargo.

*Death drops it, by default.* The grave is the recovery path and losing it for good is a real
consequence rather than a bug. `package.keep_on_death` exists because this is a family server.

**What losing it costs.** A crate is worth nothing, so the fee is not compensation for an item —
it is the only reason carrying one is a responsibility. It is **what the run would have paid on
foot** × `package.loss_multiplier`, which at the shipped 1.0 means a lost crate cancels the run
out. Deliberately *not* the actual payout: that depends on how the player travelled and is not
known until they arrive, and a price you cannot see until after you have lost the thing is a
surprise rather than a deterrent. It uses the job's **locked** distance, so the figure implied by
the board when the run was taken is the figure charged at the end of it.

*Charged for the crate, never for the failure.* The fee lands when a run ends **without the crate
coming back** — abandoned, expired, or closed any other way. A player who walks back with it still
in their bag has lost the payout, and that is the entire consequence. Handing it over is of course
free.

*Debt, and what it does and does not close.* What cannot be paid becomes debt, clamped at zero —
there is no credit with the courier office, and a negative number would turn paying one off into
banking against the next. It closes **the courier board and nothing else**. The market, the
Marketplace, the shops, the Pallets and every other way of earning stay open, because a debt that
locks a ten-year-old out of the economy with no way to work it off is the opposite of a
consequence. It is paid down at the PC, in part or in full; partial payment matters, since a debt
clearable only in one go traps somebody below the threshold indefinitely.

*A replacement, at the same price.* Bought at any PC for exactly the loss fee, which is what makes
the fee fair rather than merely punitive: without it a crate lost to a creeper four thousand blocks
out ends the run **and** charges for it, with nothing the player can do. Pricing the replacement
identically means there is no cheaper way out and no reason to prefer losing one over replacing it.
Never two crates for one job — the hand-over takes one, and the second would be an orphan wearing a
live job's id, the one case the stale-crate sweep cannot catch.

*Settled across a logout.* A run that ends with nobody there to look in cannot be settled on the
spot, so it is marked unsettled and resolved on the player's **next join** — where the question is
simply whether the crate came back with them. Without that, logging out would be a free way to lose
a crate: the cheapest and most obvious dodge, and precisely the behaviour the fee exists to
discourage. The check runs *before* the stale-crate sweep, because the sweep destroys exactly the
crates the settlement needs to see.

**Still open:** the Fragile and Perishable cargo modifiers, stubbed disabled in config.

### 3.11 The Sound Muffler
A placeable block (craft: eight wool around a note block) that hushes chosen sounds made near it. It replaces the old *Sound Muffler* mod, which players reported as broken, and does it server-side, so nobody installs anything.

**Rules.**
- **The box.** A muffler hushes sounds **made** inside its box: every block within `radius` of it in each direction (default 8, a player may pick up to `sound_muffler.max_radius`, hard limit 32). They are hushed for every listener, wherever they stand. What decides is the source's position, not the listener's.
- **Choices.** 33 friendly groups (`muffler/SoundGroups`: glob patterns over the vanilla sound key, so `entity.chicken.*` or `block.*door*`). Each is Normal, **Quieter** (the muffler's volume: 50/25/10%) or **Silent**. Single sounds can also be picked, from "Heard nearby" (what the muffler actually heard in the last 10 minutes) or a search over the sound registry. A single pick beats its group, and **Always play** is the exception to a hushed group. Where mufflers overlap, the strongest answer wins (Silent > Quieter > the lower Quieter volume). An Always play only speaks for its own muffler.
- **Ownership.** Anyone may open a muffler and see its choices. The owner or `hcm.admin` may change them. Placing needs `hcm.muffler.use` (default true). The muffler skips the economy-world sandbox (§11 #1), because quiet isn't economy.
- **Portable.** A muffler that was changed drops an item carrying its settings in PDC (`muffler_memory`), and placing it restores them.

**Mechanism** (ProtocolLib, §9.7). Paper has no sound event, so `MufflerPacketListener` watches the outgoing sound, entity-sound and level-event packets. The level events are how the dispenser click, anvils, brewing stands, grindstones, crafters and zombie door-banging reach players (`muffler/LevelEvents`). Only pure-sound events are touched; ones that also draw particles are left alone. **Silent** cancels the packet for that player. **Quieter** cancels it and queues a quieter copy, played on the next tick (`Player#playSound` with the key, category, pitch and scaled volume). A packet is never edited in place, because one packet object goes to every listener. The copy's seed carries a 24-bit mark (`muffler/Replay`) so the listener lets it pass.

**Threads and safety.** Packets leave on network threads. The listener reads only an immutable snapshot (`MufflerZones`, rebuilt and swapped in whole on every change) plus concurrent maps ("Heard nearby", the replay queue). Entity sounds are resolved only on the main thread. Any packet that can't be read passes through untouched and is logged once per kind. Pistons, fire, withers and water can't move or destroy a muffler, and explosions already skip every HomeCraft block. A 30-second sweep forgets a muffler whose block vanished without a break event.

**Entity loops: minecarts and bees** (`muffler/EntityHush`, `EntityHusher`). The minecart rumble (`MinecartSoundInstance`, and `RidingMinecartSoundInstance` for a rider) and the bee flight loops (`BeeSoundInstance`) are started by each client when it adds the entity, so no packet carries them. Checked against Mojang's 26.3 client jar: each overrides `canPlaySound()` to return `!entity.isSilent()`, the client's `SoundEngine` re-checks it every tick and stops the channel, and a stopped instance is never recreated until the client re-adds the entity. So a muffler whose effective level for the main loop key (`entity.minecart.riding`, `entity.bee.loop`: the group, or a single pick, Always play included) is **Silent**, or which has a single Silent pick of one of the kind's other loops (`entity.minecart.inside[.underwater]`, `entity.bee.loop_aggressive`), sets `Entity#setSilent(true)` (synced entity data) on those entities whose **position** is inside its box (`Muffler.contains`, as for every sound). It rescans every 10 ticks and clears the flag when they leave, the choice changes or the muffler goes. Rules:
- **Silent only.** There is no volume, so the Minecarts group steps Normal ↔ Silent and the loop keys skip Quieter.
- **TNT minecarts are never silenced.** A silent `MinecartTNT` primes without `TNT_PRIMED`, the explosion warning.
- **Only its own.** Only entities that were not already silent are silenced. They carry a `muffler_hushed` PDC tag, any tagged entity a pass finds is treated as ours, and only tagged entities are ever un-silenced.
- **Decided on add.** A tagged entity is decided on `EntityAddToWorldEvent` (start-up, chunk loads) in the same tick, before a client adds it with a stale flag: adopted if a silencing box holds it, released if not.
- **Bees and hives.** A tagged bee is released on `EntityEnterBlockEvent`, so it is stored without the flag. A bee added inside a silencing box (`CreatureSpawnEvent`, which fires before the add, for a hive exit, breeding or a spawn) is silenced there, before any client pairs it.
- **Shutdown.** Every loaded tagged entity is released. A released cart's rumble returns only when the client re-adds it (re-entering view, rejoining). Forcing a re-add with hide/show would make carts flicker and could disturb a rider, so it isn't done. This part needs no ProtocolLib.

**Out of reach, by construction:** sounds each client makes itself with no entity to silence (rain and thunder, music and jukeboxes, furnace, campfire and portal ambience, lava, a player's own footsteps, clicks and pickups). The menu says so, and no group offers a button for them.

### 3.12 Games
Games played for tokens, inside the Arcade (§3.9): five games of chance, eight menu cabinets, and time trials and mini golf in a Games world. The code is `games/` (the framework) with one package per game under `games/chance`, `games/cabinet`, `games/trial` and `games/golf`, world sessions in `games/world`, and the screens in `gui/games`. Everything sits behind `games.enabled` (shipped false). Three things work with it off too: Take a break, which also covers the older games of chance; their honest copy; and the Arcade side of `/api/arcade` (the Scratch Ticket, prizes, packs and achievements).

**The framework.** `GameCatalog.SPECS` lists the games once, in display order: the fifteen of 0.35, then the Weekly Cup, Fresh Courses, Race Night, Falling Floors and the Clubhouse. Each is a `GameSpec`: its id (the config key and the `/hcm play` id), its `GameKind` (CHANCE, CABINET, TRIAL or GOLF), the config keys it ships, its shipped settings record, a parser that clamps with one WARN per bad key and never throws, a factory, and the `ExitSettler` for rounds it leaves open. `GamesConfig` reads `games:` through these specs: junk in the common keys turns the games off, junk in one game's block closes that game, and a missing key reads as shipped. `GamesService` is always built, even with the games off, because rounds a crash left open still have to be finished. It decides whether a game is open (`games.enabled`, its block readable, its own `configEnabled()`, and not failed), holds the entry points (`/hcm play`, the Games screen, join signs) and isolates failures. Everything a game does runs inside `guard`: an exception is logged once (SEVERE, with the game id), and that one game is switched off until `/hcm reload`, with its screens closed, its world sessions ended (GAME_OFF), its OPEN rounds finished and its invites cancelled. Games never register Bukkit listeners or tasks themselves: `on`, `every` and `later` register them for the game, skip them while it is closed, run them in its guard and remove them at `stop()`. A reload leaves a game that stays open running (its `start()` is not run twice), stops the ones that closed and starts the ones that opened. `GamesHost` is the seam to the server (database, clock, config, balances, scheduler), so the framework's tests run the real service over an in-memory SQLite.

**The play gate (`PlayGate`).** One ordered gate for every game, and the order is the order a player is told things in: (0) in a world session, only that session's game; (1) the games, the game's switch, not failed; (2) `hcm.games.play`, and `hcm.games.chance` for a game of chance; (3) an economy world, a `games.worlds` world or a `games.play_worlds` world; then, for games of chance only, (4) Take a break's pause, (5) the click cooldown (silent), (6) the game's `daily_limit`, counted from today's rounds, (7) the day's token limit with this stake; and (8) the balance. Opening a screen runs 0-4; putting tokens in runs all nine; putting more in mid-round (a Double) re-runs 2, 4, 7 and 8 for the extra amount, and the pause and the limit are checked again inside the transaction that takes the tokens. Checking never starts the cooldown, so a screen can ask the gate whether to light a button; the cooldown starts when tokens go in. Going back to an OPEN round the player already paid for is not a new play and skips the gate, so a pause or a world change never leaves a paid hand to the exit rule. **Take a break** (`Breaks`) is its own small service, built right after the token service and outside the games' isolation, because it also gates the Scratch Ticket, Crates and token Card Packs (`ArcadeService.chanceRefusal`, `PackService`) with the games on or off. The day's limit is the lowest of the player's own, an admin's, and `games.chance_daily_tokens`, which counts only while the games are on, so a server with the games off behaves exactly as 0.34 for a player with no limits of their own. Tokens put in today are summed from the ledger. A lower limit applies now; a raise is stored as a pending limit with the local day it starts (`raise_delay_days`, then a midnight); a pause ends at the first local midnight at least N full days away and can only be made longer. Admin limits and pauses live in their own columns, so `clear` never touches what the player chose and `clear-own` (confirmed, logged as a WARNING) never touches what an admin set. If the row can't be read, games of chance are refused: it fails closed.

**Settling games of chance (`ChanceRounds`).** Two shapes. An **instant** round (Ore Slots, the Wheel) runs the gate, draws a seed, asks the engine for the outcome from that seed, and settles the debit, the SETTLED row and the payout in one transaction before anything is shown. An **open** round (Twenty-One, Higher or Lower) debits and writes an OPEN row carrying the seed and every payout parameter in `data`; each action is appended with `step` before its card is shown, a Double goes through `raise`, and `close` settles once, through a guarded `UPDATE … WHERE state = 'OPEN'`, so nothing pays twice. Every card is a pure function of the seed and its index, so a round is fully determined by its row. That is what the **exit settlers** use: `ExitSettler.payoutOnExit(seed, stake, data)` is pure and reads no config, and it is never better than the player's own best choice. Twenty-One stands (and takes a Double whose tokens already went in); Higher or Lower cashes out, taking the likelier offered side first if no guess was made. They run on a quit, on a join after a crash, at start for everyone offline and anything untouched for 10 minutes (`STALE_MS`), in a one-minute sweep, and when a game is switched off. A game with no settler, or one that throws, gives the tokens back. The "…was finished for you" line waits in `game_prefs` and is delivered two seconds after the next join. Coin Flip moves both players' tokens in one pair transaction. The payout is checked against `maxPayoutFor(stakes)` here too: if the capped amount would differ from what the engine decided (a bug, or a reload that lowered `max_payout` under an open screen), nothing is taken and the player is told the settings changed, because the screen showed the engine's number. A reload closes every open screen of a game of chance for the same reason; an open round stays open to resume. Ledger lines read "Ore Slots: 5 in", then "won 25" or "3 back": a partial return is never "won".

**The give-back band (`RtpLimits`, the R1.1 pick rule).** The band is [0.85, 0.95], as constants and as exact fractions. Each engine lists every return it can reach with whole-token payouts, one per candidate value of its free parameter (Stone's weight in Ore Slots, what a win adds in Twenty-One, a scale on the Wheel's bases, the growth per right guess in Higher or Lower, the winner's share in Coin Flip), and the pick is: the largest value at or below the target; if that is under 85% or there is none, the smallest value at or above 85%, as long as it is not over 95% (with one INFO line, since it lands above the target); otherwise the stake is dropped with a WARN naming the nearest values, and a game with no stake left is closed. Values are compared as exact fractions, so 17/20 counts as 85%. The engine plays and publishes the picked value, never the target: players read it floored to a whole percent ("about 89"), admins and `/api/arcade` floored to one decimal. As shipped: Ore Slots 89.9% at every stake, Twenty-One 89.7% with the best play, the Wheel 87.5/89.5/90.0% at 5/10/20, Higher or Lower 89.6/90.0/89.7% at 10/20/50, Coin Flip 90.0%.

**Skill rewards (`SkillRewards`).** `pay(player, game, source, kind, ref, tokens, gameDailyCap, detail)` never pays for a game of chance or a personal best. The `RewardKind` decides the rules the database enforces: every kind but FIRST_CLEAR and EVENT_PRIZE (Race Night's prizes) counts toward the game's `daily_cap` and `games.skill_daily_cap`, and a reward that would pass a cap pays what is left (a reward paid whole, `payWhole`, pays all or nothing: a cabinet's or Falling Floors' medal, a Fresh first finish and a Star Chart goal; `games/RewardCeilings` WARNs at load when one is bigger than a cap it counts toward); FEATURED and COURSE_OF_WEEK are once a day across all games (stored under the game `'*'`, counted toward the server-wide cap only); every kind but a personal best is one-time and carries a stable ref (`daily:<day>`, `ms:<board>:<n>`, `featured:<day>`, `first_clear:<course>`, `weekly:<course>:<week>`, `cotw:<day>`, `par:<course>:<day>`, `hio:<course>:<hole>:<day>`) that the partial unique index on `game_rewards` refuses twice. Whether the player can earn where they are (online, not creative or spectator, a world games are played in) is checked before anything one-time is recorded, so the reward waits for later. Payment is one transaction. **Scores** are one table for every game: a board is a string (`classic`, a difficulty, `daily:<day>`, `course:<id>`, `week:<id>:<week>`, `golf:<id>`); times are milliseconds, lower is better. A cabinet's daily challenge writes a DAILY row in `game_rounds` when the board is dealt, so the scored try can't be replayed; a player who can't earn where they are gets today's board as practice and keeps the try. The daily seed is HMAC-SHA256 over `day|gameId`, keyed with a random secret made once and kept in `hcm_meta` (never logged or published), so a board a player has seen can't be walked back to the secret to predict tomorrow's, as it could with a plain mixing function. Creeper Sweeper's daily board arrives dug open, so its clock starts at the deal. **Double clicks** (`gui/games/ClickHold`): a vanilla client sends a double click as two clicks and a DOUBLE_CLICK on the same slot, and a games screen repaints in place, so the extra clicks would land on whatever the first one drew. A DOUBLE_CLICK never reaches a games screen, and a screen holds clicks for 250 ms after a board is dealt into it or a "click again" is armed, and 300 ms after each Twenty-One or Higher or Lower move: a double click can't dig a board that just appeared, confirm a quit or take a card nobody saw. **Today's pick** (`Featured`) is a hash of the local day key over the open skill games and courses, or the id pinned in `games.featured`; never a game of chance. **Invites** (`Invites`) keep one pending invite per invitee, cancelled by expiry, a quit or a world change; Coin Flip invites are off until the player turns them on, friend games on until they turn them off. **Join signs** (`JoinSigns`) are tagged with the play id (`game_sign`), reformatted and waxed a tick after an admin writes them.

**World sessions (`games/world`).** Time trials and mini golf take the player into a Games world, and the player's things must come back exactly, whatever happens. The state machine is `SessionCore`, with no Bukkit types in it (every path is walked in tests through a fake port), behind `WorldSessions`. One session per player. **Entering:** close the inventory, teleport, THEN capture the state (after the teleport, so Multiverse-Inventories has already swapped in the Games world's inventory) and INSERT a `game_saved_state` row in phase **ACTIVE** (a partial unique index refuses a second live row), THEN set adventure mode, clear and give the kit. **Leaving:** bank anything that reached the player during the game into the row's `carry`, restore in place (overwriting, and only in the session's world), mark the row **RETURN** in the same tick, teleport home and hand the carry over, THEN mark it **DONE**. A RETURN row is never applied again (it only sends the player home and hands over the carry), only home is DONE, and a failed step keeps the row for the next join, `/hcm leave` or `/hcm games saved`. Whatever moves things into the carry mid-session (`bankExtras` for a hand-over, a death another plugin let happen), and every exit that banks and then fails, saves the player straight after, like every restore, so a crash can't bring them back beside the "cleared" mark to be banked twice. A snapshot goes on with no fall and no speed, and every teleport of the session's lands with none (fall damage is cancelled in a game but the fall builds, and the server keeps it through a teleport: a player sent home halfway down a drop would take it at home); a fall is spared while a trip home is under way, or in a Games world while the player has a live row (`SessionCore.sparesFall`, asked by `SessionRecoveryListener`, so with the games on, off or failed). A session let go where the player is (RETURN refused, the trip home failed, nothing could be put back) first puts them on its start (the course's start platform, the Clubhouse's arrival spot, the grid spot) by its own teleport (`SessionCore.ground`), never on the last spot a game teleported them to (a Sky Rings checkpoint is a ring in the air, a watcher's view point and put-back spots are over a course or inside the hill the watch area reaches into; that spot is only where a drop below the world goes back to), and a crash-joined watcher in a spectator mode that isn't theirs is restored at the session world's spawn, never where they were flying. A crash-join recovery that fails in place (world not loaded, snapshot or database unreadable) never leaves the player in a spectator mode that wasn't their own (a Clubhouse watcher's), and never drops them: the crash left a watcher where they were flying, so our own trip brings them down to the spawn of the world they are in first (still a spectator on the way), and only there are they put in adventure mode; a trip that fails leaves them a spectator for the next join. With the database down, only a body a game cleared (the mark in their own data) is touched. A game holds a player still (a 3-2-1) only through `WorldSessions.hold`: the move goes back where it began with the new look, and the spot is armed as the session's own first, since the server makes a changed destination a PLUGIN teleport that would otherwise read as someone else's short hop and void the player on the Go tick. DONE rows are kept 7 days for support. Nothing teleports during a disable: sessions restore in place and go to RETURN, and the players are sent home on the worlds-up pass or their next join. `SessionRecoveryListener` is registered whatever happened at start, so saved things come back even with the games off or failed. `KitGuardListener` keeps the kit in the game (it can't be dropped, stored, traded or sold, and `TokenPrizes` refuses it on every money surface) and the player safe (no damage, hunger or drops, no other screens, death cancelled, adventure mode held, a foreign teleport ending the session) and out of bystanders' reach: an effect given with any cause but PLUGIN or COMMAND (a splash potion, a cloud, an arrow, a beacon), every Paper `EntityKnockbackEvent` and a rod's CAUGHT_ENTITY pull are cancelled, so nobody can spoil a run or carry it across a gap. `GamesWorldGuard` makes the Games worlds read-only for anyone without `hcm.games.admin` while the games are on. Entities a game spawns carry `game_entity`, are never persistent and are swept on start and chunk load.

**Time trials** (`games/trial`). Parkour, elytra and boat courses live in `game_courses` (game `trials`, the course as YAML in `data`: kind, name, tier, world, start with its facing, ordered checkpoints and the finish as spheres, an optional fall height and shortest time, enabled, pinned). A course id shares the `/hcm play` namespace, so it can't be a game id, a game's alias (`blackjack`), a reserved word or another world game's course; nor `auto` (`TrialText.keptWord`), which `/hcm games feature` could never pin. `/hcm play <course>` from chat, a join sign or another player's command skips the course screen: while `warmup_seconds` is above 0 (and never on a Dropper) it first asks "Warm up (3:00)" or "Go straight to the timed run", then starts the run; a tile, the course list or today's pick opens the course screen first. The run enters a world session with the course kit (Back to checkpoint, Leave game; a worn unbreakable elytra and 3 rockets, refilled at every ring; a boat of the player's own, tagged `game_entity` and never saved); a 3-2-1 countdown holds a player on foot through the world session's hold (`WorldSessions.hold`, armed as the session's own teleport; they keep looking around) and a boat at zero speed, and restarts if they wander more than a block from the start. **Checkpoints by segment against sphere:** `Progress` tests only the NEXT target, as the straight segment between the last position and this one (`Geometry.firstHit`, a quadratic in the fraction along the move), and loops so one fast move clears every sphere it really passes through, in order, and never one it missed; the finish is the last target, and its time is interpolated to the fraction of the move where the sphere was first touched, with `System.nanoTime`, so it doesn't depend on where the tick fell. The run's own teleports are `jump`s, so nothing between two places counts. **On Fresh Courses' parkour, up on the pad** (0.37.1): on a parkour course with a `gen` tag a target counts only where the feet are no lower than `Progress.FEET_BELOW` (0.5) under its mark (`Geometry.firstHitAbove`: the move is cut to its part at or above that floor, then tested against the same sphere). A generated mark sits on its pad's top (`LiveProof` needs a solid block under every one) and its sphere reached as far below it as above, so a jump that fell short past the pad's side or under it used to count, and the fall then sent the run back onto the checkpoint it had missed. **0.37.3, over the pad** (the owner's live tests on 19 and 22 Oct: still counted): the target counts only on a move whose part inside the sphere and at or above the mark's height is also in the pad's column, within half the pad (`ParkourPlanner.CHECKPOINT_PAD` 3, `FINISH_PAD` 5) and `STAND` (0.3) of the mark across: 1.8, and 2.8 for the finish (`Geometry.firstHitOver`); or on a move whose end stands on the pad within `Progress.PAD_HAIR` (0.001) across and of the top's height, inside the sphere (`Geometry.standsOn`: a Bedrock position is the client's floats, a float step or two off, so a stand on the very edge reads 0.0004 past it and 0.00001 under the top). The column has no hair along a move: Java and Bedrock move a player down before across, so a jump stopped flat against the pad's side has its feet 0.3 past the edge only once they are under the top, while the straight line from the tick before crosses the top's height a little way out from there. A band of 0.02 along the move (the first cut) counted about one such side hit in eight, the checkpoint and the finish alike, whenever the box was within about 0.02 of the face on the tick before; the hair is only for where a move ends, and Java's jump arcs from a block's top never end a tick within it of another top, so a fall past the side never stands within it. The 2.6 sphere reached 1.1 past a checkpoint pad's edge at its top's height, so three things still counted a pad never landed on: a jump that fell short beside it above its top; one that hit its side and slid down (pinned 0.3 past the edge, the same spot as a stand on the edge, so only the height tells them apart: the floor is the top, not 0.5 under it); and on Easy, feet on the front edge of the pad before a checkpoint one step down over a 1-block gap (2.42 from the mark: `markReaches` measured to the block's edge, never the feet's 0.3 past it). The column closes all three, the pad before included (pads are at least a block apart), and lies inside the old region, so nothing counts that didn't. The time is still read where `firstHitAbove` touched the sphere in that move, so a landing's time is unchanged, and the next target is searched from where the move got over the pad (or from its end, for a stand within the hair). The rule reads the pad from the mark and the two sizes alone (no stored field), so `ParkourValidator.checkMark` refuses a mark off its pad's middle ("isn't at its pad's middle"); every ALGO 2 layout passes, so no layout, hash or `ALGO` changes. A kept parkour course (no tag) keeps the whole sphere. A reach time can only move later (about a tick on a jump up onto a pad), so neither speed check fires falsely; elytra rings, boat marks and a Dropper's pools keep the whole sphere, and `Course.Mark.contains` (safe landings) is unchanged. A hand-built parkour course (a kept one too: no tag) keeps the whole sphere: its marks are wherever the builder's feet were, and one set while flying would need a jump, or never count, with the floor under it. `trial/Footing` says whether a point is on a block (something solid under the player's width within `FEET_BELOW` under the feet): `checkpoint add` and `finish` warn when it isn't, never refuse, and `/hcm games check` WARNs each hand-built parkour course with points in the air. **Going wrong** sends the run back to its last checkpoint, facing the next, the clock still running: on parkour a fall below `fall_depth` (6) under the lower of the last and next checkpoint, or the course's own fall height on any kind; on elytra a landing or water outside the start zone (2 blocks), a checkpoint or the finish; getting out of a boat; the void; and a short teleport by someone else (a far one ends the session). **Fair play** (`FairPlay`, pure): flying, a game mode other than adventure, any potion effect, a walk speed other than 0.2, or a watched movement attribute (`movement_speed`, `jump_strength`, `step_height`, `gravity`, `safe_fall_distance`) boosted past an honest run's (`FairPlay.movement`) voids the run at Go and on every tick after. 0.37.3 judges the values Paper gives, never a modifier's key (`FairPlay.boost`): a base past the player's own (`PLAYER_BASES`), or a value past its mark (`FairPlay.mark`: `SPRINT_SPEED` 0.13, a player's own 0.1 with vanilla sprinting's +30%, for the movement speed, the player's own base for the rest), by more than `BOOST_SLACK` (1e-4 of it, a float's rounding), upward, or for `gravity` either way (`bothWays`: lighter is a longer jump or glide, heavier a faster Dropper fall or dive); a base or value that isn't a number can't be judged and voids. So a sprint under any key, powder snow, a 0 amount, a lowered base and any slow-down never void, no sprint flag is read, and a foreign boost of up to +30% passes while walking (no faster than an honest sprint) and voids once the player sprints (0.37.1's reason, through `FairPlay.finding`, now names the attribute, its value against its mark, its base and every modifier, `FairPlay.listed`; one console line a void, in a try/catch inside the tick loop, says when it fired (from the player's own Go, `TrialRun.goNanos`: a racer released after the shared Go already has time on the shared clock) and whether the player was sprinting, then everything the watch read, which `/hcm games movement [player]` also shows, each watched attribute with its own base, its mark and VOIDS; and the first time a run sees a modifier other than vanilla's sprint or powder snow on a watched attribute, `FairPlay.foreign`, the console notes it at INFO once an attribute and key, boost or not; 0.37.1's `sprintRead`, `sprintShaped`, `allowed` and sprint WARN are gone), and at the finish a run quicker than `min_seconds` (the course's own, or 5) or any leg faster than the kind allows (parkour 14, elytra 80, boat 75 blocks a second, over `max(0, d − r_prev − r_cur)`, so big spheres close together never trip it) doesn't count (`FairPlay.explained` names the leg and its speed, or the time). **Server stalls:** a move is timed when the server handles it, so moves queued during a stall arrive microseconds apart. A gap over 250 ms between two trial ticks is kept on the run as a `Stall`, running to 250 ms past the tick after it; a leg whose time overlaps one isn't speed-checked, and every other leg gets 0.05 s of slack (`LEG_SLACK`). Lag only lengthens a time, so it never voids an honest run. A voided run is shown, never recorded; a test run is judged the same way, records nothing and says whether it would have counted. **Rev and board clearing:** every geometry edit (start, a checkpoint added or removed, the finish, the fall height) bumps `rev` in the same transaction as the save and clears the course's all-time and this week's boards (`course:<id>`, `week:<id>:<week>`), after a trailing `confirm` when there are times to lose; the tier, name, shortest time and switches are not layout. Every layout edit also checks the fall height (`CourseAdmin.fallProblem`): it must sit under the course's lowest point (the start, a checkpoint or the finish), or every run goes straight back, and not under the world's floor, where no run ever falls; so `fall <y>` is refused out of that range, and so is a start, checkpoint or finish at or under an existing fall height. A run keeps a snapshot of its course and its layout fingerprint (`Course.layoutHash`: world, start, targets, fall height, in memory only), and one that finishes on a changed or deleted layout records nothing (`FairPlay.stale`), a course deleted and made again at the same `rev` included. Deleting a course clears every board of it; first clears already paid stay in `game_rewards`. A counted run goes on both boards and pays through `SkillRewards` under its kind's source (`GAMES_PARKOUR`, `GAMES_ELYTRA`, `GAMES_BOAT`): a first clear by tier (uncapped), the week's best once per course per week, the course of the week once a day (the pinned course, or a salted hash of the week key over the open courses, with the weekly quests' week), and today's pick (the course by its id, or every course while `trials` itself is pinned: `TimeTrials.featured`). The first finish is promised only while it is unpaid: the tile shows "First finish: N tokens" only then, the course screen's reward item names the amount or "done", and the finish line reads "Your first finish on …" only when that reward is being paid by this run (`TrialFinish` passes `firstFinish` to `announce`); a board a layout change emptied gives "New best!".

**Mini golf** (`games/golf`). Courses live in `game_courses` too (game `golf`): a world and up to 18 holes, each a tee with its facing, a cup block, par 2-6 and two bound corners; a course opens only when every hole is complete with its tee and cup inside its bounds. `/hcm play <course>`, a tile and a join sign all open the course screen (rules, best, record, Pick your ball, Start): only Start travels. **`BallPhysics`** is pure and deterministic, tested against a fake block grid: the ball is a point of radius 0.125 that moves once a tick in sub-steps of at most a tenth of a block, so a fast ball can't pass through a wall or over the cup. Gravity 0.04 a tick² when unsupported; sideways, each axis on its own, a solid block reflects that axis (0.6 of the speed back, slime 0.95), a half-block step is climbed only with the speed for it (and costs it), a full block bounces, a rise under 0.13 (a carpet) is rolled over; vertically it falls off edges, lands, and bounces on slime (0.75 back up; rolling onto slime pops it 0.25). Rolling friction by the block under it: 0.90 a tick normally, 0.98 on ice, 0.80 on soul sand, soul soil and honey; below 0.01 blocks a tick it stops. After every sub-step: in the cup when its centre passes within 0.3 of the cup's top centre at no more than 0.35 blocks a tick (faster, it lips out), or when it lands on the cup at any speed; or back to its last spot +1 stroke in water or lava or out of the hole's bounds (off the x/z box, or 2 blocks below its lower corner). **The cup.** "On the cup" (`Hole.onCup`) is the ball's centre within 0.5 of the cup's centre on both axes and its bottom no higher than the cup's top, nor below the cup block itself (a ball in a tunnel under a raised cup isn't in). The cup's top is the block's real collision top at its centre (`BallPhysics.cupTop` through `LiveBlocks`; 1.0 when nothing is there), read at the start of each hole with the cup's chunk loaded, so a slab cup is half a block high and the floor of a sunken hole is where the ball rests. A ball that stops on the cup is in (`restsInCup`, also at the 30-second forced stop), anywhere on a flush cup included. A ball shut in a hollow block (`trapped`: every side rises more than a climbable half-block step above it) is in only when it is over the cup and less than a block above the cup's top, i.e. a hollow block sitting on the cup; anywhere else it stays still for Reset ball. `hole <n> cup` refuses what `cupShape` calls NOTHING (no collision at the centre: air, a flower), THIN (a top under 0.2: carpet, a thin snow layer, a trapdoor laid flat, a lily pad) or HOLLOW (any sampled point more than 0.13 above the centre: a cauldron, composter or hopper), and says to look at the floor of the hole instead. The clubs, Tap to Drive, send it at 0.20, 0.38, 0.60, 0.90 and 1.30 blocks a tick the way the player looks, from within 4 blocks, once it is still. `GolfRun` counts strokes and penalties and picks a hole up at par + `max_over_par`, scoring exactly that. **`BallView`** (R3.13) draws the ball with two non-persistent, `game_entity`-tagged entities moved together every tick: an `ItemDisplay` at half scale with a two-tick teleport interpolation for Java players, and a small invisible marker armour stand wearing the ball, hidden from everyone and shown to each Bedrock player within 64 blocks (Geyser can't draw display entities), who get the display hidden instead. The ball item is a fresh plain head with the texture of a Mini the player owns (picked on "Pick your ball", kept in `game_prefs` as `golf.ball`), never the Mini's own item or tag; a plain white ball when there is none, and a white block for a Bedrock player. Both are swept at start and on chunk load. Layout edits (a tee, cup, bounds, adding or removing a hole) bump `rev` and clear `golf:<id>`, after a `confirm` when there are scores; par and the name are not layout. A layout edit also ends the rounds on that course at once (`closeCourse` with `GolfRounds.changedLine`, reason ADMIN): a round on the old layout can't count, so the player goes home with their things and is told why. A finished round on an unchanged, open course is recorded and pays, while the player is still in the session: a first clear (uncapped), par or better once per course per day, each hole-in-one once per hole per day (only in a finished round, celebrated "Hole in one!" with `BigWin`'s title and firework, a skill game's moment), and today's pick (the course, or `golf` pinned as a whole). A round on a changed, closed or deleted course, or one left early, records nothing (`GolfRounds.stale` checks the row's `rev`, game and `enabled`); a layout edit, closing or deleting a course sends its players home, and `begin` sends home a player still on the way to the first tee when the course was closed, changed or deleted meanwhile. Every saved golf edit is logged at INFO with who made it (`Games: <admin> <action> golf course <id> - <detail>`), and a command that throws is logged SEVERE and answered "That didn't work - see the console." rather than reaching the guard that would switch golf off. Several players can play one course, each with their own ball; balls don't collide.

**The restart hold (`RestartHold`).** The host restarts the server at fixed local times (`games.restart_times`, shipped `["04:00", "16:00"]`) and warns players itself, so the games add no warnings; for the last `restart_hold_minutes` (5) before each restart they only stop new things a restart would cut off. The hold is `[restart - hold, restart + 1 minute)`: a time must be the minute the host actually stops the server, not when its warnings start, and a host stops it some seconds into that minute, so the minute itself is held too (`RestartHold.RESTART_MINUTE_MS`; `next()` returns that restart until its minute is over, so the arena's `restartClose`, the Fresh Courses scheduler's `nearRestart`/`abandon` and Race Night's fit all see it). A plugin enabled inside that minute came back from that restart: `GamesService` hands its enable time to every hold it builds, and `next()` skips the restart that time fell in (`RestartHold.cameBackFrom`). `WorldSessions.enter` refuses a course or golf round ("The server restarts at 4:00 PM. New runs open again after it."), taking and moving nothing; `ChanceRounds.open` refuses a new Twenty-One or Higher or Lower hand before the debit, while an OPEN hand resumes and settles as normal; `CabinetGame.startDaily` refuses the same way (returns null, deals nothing, writes no attempt row) to a player whose scored try is unused. Not practice on today's seed, as the "can't earn here" path does: that would show every player the day's layout minutes before their scored try. Instant plays finish at once, and Classic runs and practice after a used try have no daily try to lose, so none of them is held. `RestartHold` is pure (times, `clock.time_zone`, the hold, "now" from `GameClock`) and works date by date through `ZonedDateTime`, so the times stay on the wall clock across DST: a time a spring-forward day skips happens at the first instant after the gap, a repeated one happens once, and a 00:02 restart holds from 23:57 the day before.

**The feed.** `GET /api/arcade` (`web/ArcadeFeed`) is built on the main thread with the other feeds and served cached. `ArcadeFeed` is the games' `FeedWriter`: every open game writes its own entries from the same settings and engine it plays with, inside its guard (a game whose feed throws is switched off and what it wrote is dropped, never the feed), and the writer owns every format and omission rule: RTPs floored to one decimal, `chance` to 4 significant digits with the screen's `oneIn`, a stake only with its RTP, a paytable row only with its odds, an empty section left out. The Scratch Ticket's entry comes with its pot, so the site never shows one without the other, and the achievement won by a game of chance (`jackpot`) is never advertised. No player data: records carry a score or a time and a date, and a `holder` only while `web.dashboard.arcade_show_names` is true (shipped false).

**Fresh Courses: paying and showing a set.** A generated course's run pays and shows by the set (edition) its layout belongs to, never the calendar: its time goes on `gfresh:<slot>:<edition>` (a recalled course's is its original set's, so its old records are the ones to beat), its stars on `GenBoards.stars(tag)` (a recall's own board) and into the Star Chart of the week the run is played in (`edition.weekKey(edition.day(now))`, whatever day its set began), its first finish of the set under `fresh:<slot>:<edition>` (the reroll dropped, so a reroll pays none) at `dailyClear(slot, tag.cadence())`, so a set kept over a cadence change pays by what it is; golf's par and holes-in-one once per set; the first clear once per slot, never per Classics row. Star Chart goals come only from the engine's fixed list for the week (`goals(week)`, kept in `hcm_meta` the first time they are handed out), each paying its own tokens. **All or nothing:** FRESH_CLEAR and goal tokens go through `SkillRewards.payWhole` (`GamesDao.payReward(..., whole)`): when the caps left today can't pay the whole amount, nothing is paid or written, so the once-only ref is still free on another day of the set; every other reward keeps partial pay. So a reward paid whole must fit every cap it counts toward (one bigger than a whole day's cap pays that cap, once): no shipped amount is above one (since 0.37, Hard Parkour's weekly 20 and Golf of the Week's 25 under caps of 40 and 60), and `RewardCeilings` WARNs at load about one that is. Every screen word about a set comes from `GenCopy` by the set's cadence ("this week", "today", "on this course"); "the current set" is the engine's (`GenService.today(slot)`), not a date sum. `FreshMenu` (the renamed Today's Courses) shows the set, the four Classics (Classic Parkour, Classic Sky Rings, Classic Golf and Classic Dropper) (holding one, being built, or empty, from `classic(id)` and `classicPending(id)`) and the Star Chart; a course's code (`GenService.code(tag)`) ends every generated tile's and course screen's NAME and follows the finish line.

**Fresh Courses: the archive, recall and keep.** The archive row (`gen_editions`, with its course code) is written in the flip's transaction, so every set that was ever live has one. Rebuilding always uses the stored plan (PlanCodec v1, gzipped), never the planner, unless an admin asks for `seed:` (then marked "(re-made)"). A recall is a Classics slot's wanted state in `hcm_meta` (`gen.<classic>.recall`), which the engine converges like a due build; a recalled course's tag is the original set's (the original board and FRESH_CLEAR ref), its stars its own. A keep writes `gen.keep.pending` before its first block and registers the normal row (plus the copied records, `kept_as` and the plot) in one transaction after verify. A free plot, unlike a half, is unguarded hand-built territory, so it is never "claimed": every keep scans its plot empty first (only a keep resumed from `gen.keep.pending` skips that), and no plot job runs while keeping is off, over another plot's kept course, or near a registered course; plot jobs wait for the live courses' boot checks and start none in the restart window; a re-made keep's plan has the engine's 120 s kill and the online throttle; the generator never writes into a used plot again; one kept course per set.

**Fresh Courses: where the places stand, and what players can see (LAYOUT-SPEC §1, LAYOUT-DECISIONS).** The owner's words: "it will look really weird if you can see the other stuff around you floating". *The rule* (`games/gen/api/Sight`, pure): the server sends a player every chunk column within its view distance V plus one (vanilla's rounded rule, bounded by the square "Chebyshev distance <= V + 1", a superset, so it errs safe), and a player can be up to 16 blocks outside a place (watching grows a half by 8, a kept course's watch area is its marks + 16), so D(P, Q) is the chunk columns between P grown by 16 and Q, x and z only (whole columns are sent, so height never helps; `Box.gap` counts y and is never used for sight), and Q is out of P's sight exactly when D >= V + 2. The layout is designed for V = 32, Paper's largest, because then no setting the owner (or a host panel, or `World#setViewDistance`) can choose brings the floating view back; the cost is only sky, since every trip between places is a teleport and the sky between them is never loaded. So **every place is 576 blocks (36 chunk columns) from every other**, a course's own spare half included (`Slots.HALF_GAP`, per slot `half_gap`; `KeepArea.DEFAULT_GAP`, `keep.plot_gap`): D = 36, out of sight up to V = 34, two chunks to spare. `Regions.APART` (32) is split from it and stays the *build* rule (closer than that, the later place isn't built); sight is only ever a WARN, since a course that can see another still works. *The spots* (§1.5): the nine courses and four Classics in two columns (x 6080 and 7488, each in a row of its own, z 4096..7967), the Clubhouse and Falling Floors at z 8544, the kept plots west from x 1760, z 7296; everything in x 1760..8191, z 4096..10367, y 128..303 (0.35's heights), which needs a world border at least 21,000 across (each half and each kept plot is checked against the border, height, spawn and `safe_spot`; the keep area had no such check in 0.35). All 26 halves stay in x, z 4096..8191, where 0.35's halves stood: golf's ball, the Dropper's pilots and the elytra sim add and floor absolute doubles, and a whole-block shift inside one power-of-two range gives bit-identical answers (a probe of 184 plans agreed hash for hash); kept plots sit outside it, measured at no difference. `ShippedLayoutTest` pins the defaults and the bundled config.yml to the table and proves no pair in sight at V = 32 for the 26 halves, both extras and 100 plots, against a brute force over every chunk; the planner goldens stand at explicit 0.35 boxes (`LegacyBoxes`), so moving the shipped spots moved no golden. A claim names its gap when it isn't 32 (8 fields; 7 fields always mean 32), so a changed gap is a moved region exactly as a changed origin is. *The void world* (`games/VoidWorld`): the plugin's `getDefaultWorldGenerator` is an empty generator (no noise, surface, caves, decorations, mobs or structures, `the_void` biomes) with a fixed spawn on a 5 x 5 smooth-stone platform and a light at (0, 100, 0), each block placed by the chunk it falls in; `/mv create <name> normal -g HomeCraftManagement`. Bukkit hands a world only the generator of a plugin that is already enabled, and Multiverse loads its worlds while it enables, at every start: so plugin.yml has `loadbefore: [Multiverse-Core]` (and neither Multiverse plugin as a soft dependency, which would be a load loop), else the world would get vanilla terrain in every chunk made after the first restart (`PluginLoadOrderTest`). What going first changes is handled: nothing in `onEnable` needs a Multiverse world (the Games wait for theirs; a courier site whose world isn't loaded yet but is on disk waits for its chunks, `BuildingService.worldGone`); the join handlers that look at the player's world or bag (Courier, Arcade tokens) run at HIGH, after Multiverse's NORMAL ones; and Multiverse-Inventories, which saves every player's things as it disables, now disables first, so the world sessions end at its `PluginDisableEvent` (`SessionCore.disabling`: in place while the server stops), and it never saves a game's kit. *Nothing moves by itself.* The spec's automatic relocation (copy, prove, flip, tidy, rollback, about 3,500 lines rewriting the engine under course variety) only helps a 0.35 install whose areas are already built, and none exists; skipping it is a scope decision, not a risk, because such an install is never harmed: it keeps its spots and is told how to move by hand (`clear` first, then the origin: README "Moving an area by hand"; a slot cleared first, or a Classic closed with `unrecall` and emptied, is moved with an INFO, not 0.35's "not cleared" WARN, and a Classic moved while it holds a recall is told to close it first, since `clear` doesn't take a Classic). *No value that can't be read is a move* (`SlotConfig.placed`): a course's or Classic's origin or `half_gap` that can't be read leaves it unplaced, off whatever an admin's `on` says and held by the engine at its claim, so a typo never rerolls it elsewhere and fixing the typo changes nothing; a bad switch or tier keeps the spot config gives; an unreadable Clubhouse or arena origin closes that block (the Clubhouse's other bad values still fall back and stay open). None falls back to the shipped spot, which on a server that kept 0.35's spots is where nothing stands. *What players can see* (`/hcm games check`, `command/SightCheck`, pure, after the Clubhouse): each Games world void or not and a WARN for a spawn over nothing, the V really used (the largest of the world's view and send distances and every online player's send distance there, since a plugin may raise one player's), then each pair of places in sight, nearest first, at most 8, each with how to move one by hand and the view distance that would hide it; never through an area's `problems` (those are FAILs). The places are the courses' and Classics' halves, the kept plots, the Clubhouse, the arena, and as points the world's spawn, `safe_spot` and each Race Night stand an admin set for a kept or hand-built course (with a spectator's reach, since it can be set anywhere: its fix is `/hcm games event stand <id> set` nearer its course); two points hold nothing built, so they are never a pair nor "the closest two places", and neither is a stand and its own kept course. `Sight.within` is the one "in sight at V" rule both `inSight` and the pairs use. The kept-plot WARN is one line per kind of problem (`Regions.PlotIssue`: border, spawn, `safe_spot`, height), each with the fix that fits it.

**Fresh Courses: the legacy guard (`games/gen/LayoutGuard`, LAYOUT-DECISIONS item 5).** The new spots and gaps must never reach a server that built at 0.35's: the engine reads a changed origin or gap as a moved area (a reroll onto a fresh board, the old blocks left standing, a Dropper's water guarded until drained), and the Clubhouse and the arena would build a second room. So a server where anything was built (any Fresh set or live claim, a Classic holding a recall, a kept course or one being kept, the Clubhouse or the arena claimed, and to be safe a slot's old wet halves, an archived set or a Fresh course row) keeps every 0.35 spot and shape; one where nothing was takes the new layout, except an owner's own origin, which stays with `half_gap: 32` (or `plot_gap: 0`) so its shape stays too, and an untouched default whose new spot would crowd an owner's spot, which keeps its 0.35 one. *Why two steps:* whether anything was built is in the database, and config.yml is migrated before `database.connect()`. Config revision 19 therefore only marks a file that came from an older version (a top-level `games_layout_check` section, so a file from before the Games keeps the backfill's commented Games block, noting which places had no origin yet: on a server that keeps 0.35's layout the value the backfill then writes for those goes back to 0.35's, while a new value an owner typed stays theirs), and the guard runs in `onEnable` right after the database opens and before anything reads the Games settings (and in `/hcm reload`, before the settings are read again), decides, writes config.yml the house's careful way (read defaults-free from disk, snapshot, save), and stamps the decision in `hcm_meta` (`gen.layout.guard` = `legacy|new`, when, and what counted as built). *Why it is crash-safe:* decide, then write the file (the mark goes in the same write), then stamp; the database doesn't change before the stamp, so a stop anywhere before it decides the same again, and applying a decision to a file that already has it changes nothing (so the next start writes nothing); after the stamp there is nothing to do. The Games start only once the guard has finished (a failure is SEVERE and leaves them off for that start; at a reload the running Games keep the settings they had), so nothing is ever built before the stamp: "no stamp" always means "whatever is built, 0.35 built". A marked file that comes back later (a restored config.yml) gets the stamped decision, never a new one. A file still below revision 19 (the migration couldn't save it, so it was never marked) counts as marked: the decision must be written into it before anything is stamped, so a read-only file keeps the Games off rather than start them on 0.35's origins at the new gaps (which the next, marked, start would then move). A stamp that was lost is told by where things were built (`LayoutGuard.builtNew`): a claim of this version's shape (8 fields, the Clubhouse or arena at its new box, a plot of the new keep area) is one 0.35 never wrote, so the decision is NEW, and a file already through revision 19 is left exactly as it is (only a marked file with nothing at an old spot is moved to the new spots again). A stamp that holds something else stops the run and names the word to write back (read from the file's shape), never "delete the row". An origin 0.35 couldn't read gets 0.35's own on LEGACY (0.35 fell back to its shipped spot, so it built there). `/hcm config reset games[.<part>]` leaves every spot path (`LayoutGuard.spotPaths`: origins, `half_gap`, `keep.area`, `keep.plot_gap`, a Classic's bare list whole) and the Games worlds (`games.worlds`, `games.fresh.world`) exactly as on disk, on every server, and the dry run lists them as kept (`ConfigReset.kept`): putting a bundled one back is a move without a clear (0.35's spots on a legacy server, a place moved by hand, a void world), and places move by hand only. The config read before `database.connect()` parses the Games settings quietly while the file carries the mark (`PluginConfig.games`): its 0.35 origins at the new gaps would WARN of Classics on top of each other that aren't; the file is read again, aloud, once the guard has written it. The rules err towards "built": a server wrongly counted as built only keeps the old layout and a WARN; one wrongly counted as empty would lose its courses' boards and leave their blocks. `LayoutGuardUpgradeTest` walks it start by start through the real migration and backfill (a fresh install, a 0.34 file, a 0.35 file never built, each kind of built thing, an owner's spot, a second start, a stop between every two steps, a restored file), `LegacyLayoutEngineTest` shows on the real engine that courses 0.35 built stay claimed, healed and on the same editions after it (and that the same start without it takes the move path for every one), and `LayoutGuardMutationTest` drops each rule of the built-detection in turn, and all of them, and needs a scenario to fail every time.

<!-- ---- Course Variety (COURSE-VARIETY-SPEC, COURSE-VARIETY-DECISIONS) ---- -->
**Course Variety: the groundwork (COURSE-VARIETY-SPEC §0.4, §1).** The owner's words: "I hope the races aren't just flat races... and fun things like sand pits or trees... not just a flat circle, or a dogleg left that is flat." Two planners go to algo 3, `GolfPlanner` (Adventure Golf) and `BoatPlanner` (the Mountain Run); nothing else changes shape. *The rules every part keeps:* a generated course is accepted only when validators that read the plan's blocks prove it (a simulator that isn't the game's own code may only reject a course, never prove one: R1); `BallPhysics` doesn't change (R3); every seed gives a course, through a tested fallback (`SAFE_STRAIGHT`, the new `SAFE_SPIRAL`: R4); a layout is always judged by the rules of the algo it was made with, never today's (R5: `BoatValidator` sends algo 2 and older to `LoopValidatorV2`, `GolfValidator` keeps its algo-2 checks private and frozen and sends algo 3 on to `GolfValidatorV3`, both pinned by serialised algo-2 fixtures, and the planner goldens stand at explicit 0.35 boxes, `LegacyBoxes`); and no config key shapes a layout (R6: a layout comes only from algo, seed, day, reroll, tier or mix and half, because pins and picks remember only the algo, mix and fall depth, rederive rebuilds from the tag and the Weekly Cup keys on the plan hash), so piece decks, quotas and palettes are constants of the algo. *The palette* (`Palette`, pinned by `PaletteTest`) gains `smooth_sandstone` and its bottom slab (sand that never falls), `moss_block` (grass that never spreads), `oak|birch|cherry_log[axis=y]` and `..._leaves[distance=d,persistent=true,waterlogged=false]` with the exact distance vanilla would give each leaf (`Palette.leafDistances`; `stateProblems`/`leafProblems` refuse anything else), so a verify pass never sees a canopy change and leaves never decay. *Water* is golf's now too, through one predicate at every gate: `Slots.Def.mayHoldWater() = dropper() || golf()` (`PlanCheck` admits still sources only for it; `GenService` builds such a pass in the staged order, water last and drained first, drains it before a clear, and keeps a moved area's old halves guarded as wet, "drain first"; `KeepService` and the keep plots' flow guard likewise; `LiveProof` checks it at boot); the ice boat stays dry. `Pools` is the shared sealed-pool rule (water only as still sources inside declared pool boxes, at most `maxDepth` deep, golf 1, every side and below water or a *full* block: a slab, sign or leaves can hold water, so they never seal), and `LiveProof.pools` its twin on the real blocks, with the plan's own seal test (`Pools.seals`), so a real block with extra states seals as the plan did.
